I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If multiple elements have the same highest frequency (tie), we should return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's a unique majority

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time → A is majority
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times → tie → return None

### Step 2: Algorithm Design
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if exactly one element has this maximum frequency
4. If yes, return that element; if no (tie), return None

### Step 3: Implementation Strategy
- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Iterate through the list to build frequency map
- Find the maximum frequency
- Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
- If exactly one element has maximum frequency, return it; otherwise return None

### Step 4: Edge cases to consider
- Empty list → return None
- All elements same → return that element
- All elements different → return None (tie)

## Code Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count == max_freq:
            max_count += 1
            majority_element = element
    
    # If there's exactly one element with maximum frequency, return it
    # Otherwise return None (tie)
    return majority_element if max_count == 1 else None
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Handle empty list case
2. Count frequencies using a dictionary
3. Find the maximum frequency value
4. Count how many elements achieve this maximum frequency
5. Return the element if exactly one element has the maximum frequency, otherwise return None

Time complexity: O(n) where n is the length of the array
Space complexity: O(k) where k is the number of unique elements in the array

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If multiple elements have the same highest frequency (tie), we should return None
3. If there's only one element with the highest frequency, we return that element
4. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's a clear majority

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Count frequencies of each element
- Use a dictionary to count how many times each string appears in the list

### Step 2: Find the maximum frequency
- Determine what the highest frequency is

### Step 3: Check if there's a unique majority
- Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
- If only one element has the maximum frequency, return that element
- If multiple elements have the maximum frequency, return None

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- Empty list - should return None
- All elements same - should return that element

## Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    # Handle empty array
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
    max_elements = [element for element, count in freq.items() if count == max_freq]
    
    # If only one element has the maximum frequency, return it
    if len(max_elements) == 1:
        return max_elements[0]
    else:
        return None
```

## Verification with examples:

1. `majority(["A", "B", "A"])`:
   - Frequencies: A:2, B:1
   - Max frequency: 2
   - Only "A" has frequency 2 → returns "A" ✓

2. `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])`:
   - Frequencies: A:2, B:2
   - Max frequency: 2
   - Both "A" and "B" have frequency 2 → returns None ✓

The solution correctly handles the problem requirements.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If no element appears more than half the time (i.e., there's no majority), return None

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze what "majority" means
A majority element must appear more than n/2 times where n is the total number of elements. This means it needs to appear at least floor(n/2) + 1 times.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the element with the highest frequency
3. Check if that frequency is greater than n/2
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

### Step 3: Implementation details
- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Iterate through the array once to count
- Find the maximum frequency and the corresponding element
- Check if max frequency > len(arr) / 2

### Step 4: Edge cases
- Empty list: should return None
- All elements the same: should return that element
- All elements appear equally: should return None

Let me trace through the examples:
- ["A", "B", "A"]: A appears 2 times, total is 3, 2 > 3/2 = 1.5 → A is majority
- ["A", "B", "B", "A"]: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times, total is 4, 2 is not > 4/2 = 2 → no majority → None

## Code Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the element with maximum frequency
    max_freq = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > max_freq:
            max_freq = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if this is actually a majority (appears more than half the time)
    n = len(arr)
    if max_freq > n / 2:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None
```

The solution works by:
1. Handling empty array case
2. Counting frequencies using a dictionary
3. Finding the element with maximum frequency
4. Checking if that frequency exceeds half the array length (majority condition)
5. Returning the element if it's a majority, otherwise None

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If multiple elements have the same highest frequency (tie), we should return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])` → returns "A" (A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time, A has majority)
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])` → returns None (A and B both appear 2 times, tie)

### Step 2: Algorithm Design
1. Count frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if any element has frequency strictly greater than half the total length
4. If yes, return that element; if no (tie or no clear majority), return None

### Step 3: Implementation Details
- Use a dictionary or Counter to count frequencies
- Calculate the "majority threshold" = (length of list + 1) // 2
- An element is a majority if its count > threshold

### Step 4: Code Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(frequency.values())
    
    # Calculate the threshold for majority
    # For a majority, an element needs to appear more than half the time
    # So threshold is (len(arr) // 2) + 1
    threshold = len(arr) // 2 + 1
    
    # Check if any element has frequency greater than threshold
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if count > threshold:
            return element
    
    # No clear majority found
    return None
```

## Verification with Examples

Example 1: `["A", "B", "A"]`
- Frequencies: A=2, B=1
- Threshold = 3//2 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2
- A has count 2 > 2? No, 2 is not greater than 2
- Wait, this is wrong. Let me reconsider.

Actually, for majority, we need an element to appear more than half the time. In a list of length 3, more than half means more than 1.5, so at least 2.

So threshold should be (len(arr) // 2) + 1.

For length 3: 3//2 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
For length 4: 4//2 + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 ✓

Example 1: A appears 2 times, threshold is 2, so 2 > 2 is false. But we want 2 > 1.5, which means 2 >= 2.

Actually, let me reconsider the definition. "More than half" means > n/2.

For n=3, half is 1.5, so > 1.5 means 2 or more.

So we need count > n/2, which means count >= floor(n/2) + 1.

In code: count > len(arr) / 2

But since we're dealing with integers, we can use: count > len(arr) / 2

Or equivalently: count > len(arr) // 2 + (1 if len(arr) % 2 == 1 else 0) - this is complex.

Simpler: count > len(arr) / 2

But we need to avoid floating point. We can use: count * 2 > len(arr)

This is equivalent to count > len(arr) / 2.

Let me fix the threshold:

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # For majority, we need count > len(arr) / 2
    # This is equivalent to 2 * count > len(arr)
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if 2 * count > len(arr):
            return element
    
    # No clear majority found
    return None
```

## Final Solution

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # For majority, we need count > len(arr) / 2
    # This is equivalent to 2 * count > len(arr)
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if 2 * count > len(arr):
            return element
    
    # No clear majority found
    return None
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If no single element appears more than half the time, return None

## Key Insight

For a "majority" element, it must appear more than n/2 times where n is the length of the list. This means it must appear at least n//2 + 1 times.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the element with the highest frequency
3. Check if that frequency exceeds n/2 (majority threshold)
4. Return the element if it's a majority, otherwise return None

## Algorithm

- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Iterate through the array and count each element
- Find the element with maximum count
- Check if max_count > len(arr) / 2
- Return the element if majority exists, otherwise return None

## Edge Cases

- Empty list: should return None
- All elements same: should return that element
- Tied frequencies: no majority, return None

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the element with maximum frequency
    max_freq = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > max_freq:
            max_freq = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if this is a majority (appears more than half the time)
    n = len(arr)
    if max_freq > n // 2:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If there's no clear majority (no element appears more than half the time), return None

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time. Total elements = 3. 2/3 > 1/2, so A is majority → return "A"
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times. Total elements = 4. No element appears more than 4/2 = 2 times. Both appear exactly half the time, so no clear majority → return None

## Algorithm Design

1. Count frequency of each element using a dictionary or Counter
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if any element has frequency greater than n/2 (where n is the total length)
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

## Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    n = len(arr)
    
    # Check if any element appears more than half the time
    # (more than n/2 elements)
    if max_freq > n / 2:
        # Find which element has the maximum frequency
        for element, count in freq.items():
            if count == max_freq:
                return element
    
    return None
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Handle empty array edge case
2. Count frequency of each element using a dictionary
3. Find the maximum frequency
4. Check if any element appears more than n/2 times (this is what defines a majority)
5. Return that element if found, otherwise return None

This correctly handles both examples and ensures we only return a value when it truly appears more than half the time.

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If no single element appears more than half the time (i.e., there's a tie for most frequent), return None
3. The majority element must appear more than n/2 times where n is the length of the array

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time. 2 > 3/2 = 1.5, so A is majority
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times. No element appears > 4/2 = 2 times, so no majority

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Count frequency of each element using a dictionary or Counter
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if the maximum frequency is greater than n/2
4. If yes, return the element with that frequency; otherwise return None

### Step 3: Implementation considerations
- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Iterate through the array to build the frequency map
- Find the element with maximum frequency
- Check if max frequency > len(arr)/2
- Return appropriate value

### Step 4: Edge cases
- Empty array: should return None
- All elements same: should return that element
- Tied frequencies: should return None

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    max_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > max_count:
            max_count = count
            max_element = element
    
    # Check if the maximum frequency is greater than n/2
    n = len(arr)
    if max_count > n // 2:
        return max_element
    else:
        return None
```

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Check if any element appears more than half the length of the array
    # (majority means appears more than n/2 times)
    n = len(arr)
    majority_threshold = n // 2 + 1
    
    # Find the element with maximum frequency that meets the majority threshold
    candidates = [element for element, count in freq.items() if count >= majority_threshold]
    
    if candidates:
        return candidates[0]
    else:
        return None
```

def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Check if any element appears more than half the length of the array
    # (majority means appears more than n/2 times)
    n = len(arr)
    majority_threshold = n // 2 + 1
    
    # Find the element with maximum frequency that meets the majority threshold
    candidates = [element for element, count in freq.items() if count >= majority_threshold]
    
    if candidates:
        return candidates[0]
    else:
        return None

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if cell (i,j) has a 1, then cell (j,i) must also have a 1)
4. The resulting matrix should be lexicographically maximal
5. If it's impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For any cell (i,j), if we place a 1, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair requires 2 ones, and diagonal cells require only 1 one.

2. **Lexicographically maximal**: To maximize lexicographically, we want to place 1s as early as possible in the row-major ordering. This means we should fill the matrix from top-left to bottom-right.

3. **Feasibility check**: 
   - For diagonal positions (i,i): each requires 1 one
   - For off-diagonal positions (i,j) where i < j: each pair (i,j) and (j,i) requires 2 ones
   - So if we place a ones at diagonal positions and b pairs of off-diagonal positions, we need: a + 2b = k

4. **Optimal strategy**: To maximize lexicographically, we should:
   - First place 1s on the diagonal (they don't require symmetry pairs)
   - Then place 1s in symmetric pairs to maximize the top-left part

## Algorithm

1. First, check if k is impossible: k must be achievable as a sum of diagonal ones (each costs 1) and symmetric pairs (each costs 2).

2. For lexicographic maximality, we want to place 1s in the earliest possible positions:
   - Fill diagonal positions first (they can be placed individually)
   - Then fill symmetric pairs (i,j) where i < j, starting from top-left

3. However, since we want lexicographic maximality, we need to fill from top-left to bottom-right, so we should:
   - For each row from 0 to n-1, try to place 1s in columns from 0 to n-1
   - But due to symmetry, when we place at (i,j), we also place at (j,i)

4. Better approach:
   - We can place at most n ones on the diagonal (positions (0,0), (1,1), ..., (n-1,n-1))
   - For off-diagonal, we can place pairs: each pair (i,j) where i < j costs 2 ones
   - So we need k ≤ n + 2 * (number of pairs)
   - Maximum pairs possible is C(n,2) = n*(n-1)/2

2. If k > n*n, return -1

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k exceeds the maximum possible ones (n*n)
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix initialized with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Iterate through upper triangle (including diagonal) in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if remaining == 0:
                break
            if i == j:  # diagonal element
                cost = 1
            else:  # off-diagonal symmetric pair
                cost = 2
                
            if cost <= remaining:
                matrix[i][j] = 1
                matrix[j][i] = 1
                remaining -= cost
    
    # If we couldn't place exactly k ones (shouldn't happen given the constraints)
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Wait, I just realized: the condition `if k > n*n` ensures we don't go beyond maximum, and since we can always achieve any value from 0 to n*n due to the parity and availability of diagonal and symmetric pairs, we shouldn't need the final check.

But is it always possible to achieve any k from 0 to n*n?

Actually, we can achieve any k where k ≤ n*n, because:
- We can place diagonal ones (cost 1 each) and symmetric pairs (cost 2 each)
- We have n diagonal positions and (n*(n-1)//2) symmetric pairs
- The total capacity is n + 2*(n*(n-1)//2) = n*n
- We can achieve all values from 0 to n*n by combining these, since 1 and 2 are coprime.

For example, to achieve any value m, we can:
- Start with 0 ones
- Add 1 ones at diagonal
- Add 2 ones at symmetric pairs

Since 1 and 2 generate all integers ≥ 0, we can achieve any m from 0 to n*n as long as we have enough capacity.

Therefore, the only constraint is k ≤ n*n.

So we can remove the final check.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k exceeds the maximum possible ones (n*n)
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix initialized with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Iterate through upper triangle (including diagonal) in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if remaining == 0:
                break
            if i == j:  # diagonal element
                cost = 1
            else:  # off-diagonal symmetric pair
                cost = 2
                
            if cost <= remaining:
                matrix[i][j] = 1
                matrix[j][i] = 1
                remaining -= cost
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (so if there's a 1 at position (i,j), there must be a 1 at position (j,i))
4. The resulting matrix should be lexicographically maximal
5. If no such matrix exists, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every cell (i,j) where i ≠ j, if we place a 1, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means we need to place 2 ones for each off-diagonal position.

2. **Diagonal positions**: For positions where i = j (main diagonal), placing a 1 only requires one "slot".

3. **Lexicographic maximality**: To achieve lexicographic maximality, we should try to place ones as early as possible in the row-wise order (from top to bottom, left to right).

4. **Feasibility check**: 
   - If k is too large, it might be impossible due to the symmetry constraint
   - The maximum number of ones we can place is: n (on diagonal) + 2 × (number of off-diagonal pairs)
   - The total maximum is n + 2 × (n×(n-1)/2) = n + n×(n-1) = n²

5. **By symmetry**: 
   - We can only place ones in pairs (i,j) and (j,i) for i ≠ j, or on the diagonal at (i,i)
   - The total number of ones must satisfy: k ≤ n²

## Strategy

1. First, check if k > n². If yes, return -1.

2. To be lexicographically maximal, we should try to place ones in the top-left corner first, then move right and down.

3. We have two types of positions:
   - Diagonal positions (i,i): each can hold 1 (cost: 1)

Let me think differently:

Actually, since the matrix is symmetric, we can represent it by considering the upper triangle (including diagonal).

For position (i,j) where i <= j:
- If i == j: cost = 1
- If i < j: cost = 2

And for lexicographic maximality, we should fill positions in order (i,j) as we go through row-major order.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is too large
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # We need to place k ones
    # We'll go through positions in row-major order (i, j) where i <= j
    # For diagonal (i,i): cost = 1
    # For off-diagonal (i,j) with i < j: cost = 2
    
    remaining = k
    
    # Go through each position in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):  # j >= i, so we cover upper triangle including diagonal
            if i == j:
                # Diagonal position: place 1 if we have enough budget
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
                # If we don't have budget, we skip
            else:
                # Off-diagonal position: placing costs 2 ones
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1  # symmetry
                    remaining -= 2
                # If we don't have budget, we skip
    
    # If we still have remaining ones, it means we couldn't place exactly k ones
    # But since we've checked k <= n*n, and we've placed at most n*n ones, 
    # and we're going in lex order, we should have placed exactly k ones?
    
    # Actually, our algorithm might not place exactly k ones if we can't place in a way that respects symmetry.
    # But we've checked k <= n*n, so it should be possible?
    
    # However, consider: we need to place k ones. The total number of ones we can place is at most n*n.
    # But we might not be able to place exactly k if k is not achievable with the pattern.
    
    # Actually, we can always achieve any k from 0 to n*n with symmetry constraint?
    # No, not quite. For example, if k is odd and n=1, then k=1 is possible, k=3 is impossible (3>1).
    
    # But we already checked k <= n*n.
    
    # However, what about the pairing constraint? 
    # We can achieve any k between 0 and n*n, because:
    # - We can place 0 ones
    # - We can add 1 at diagonal
    # - For any even number, we can add pairs
    # - For odd numbers, we can add diagonals
    
    # Since k <= n*n and we can achieve any value from 0 to n*n by appropriately choosing diagonals and pairs, 
    # the answer always exists when k <= n*n.
    
    # Therefore, we just need to output the matrix.
    
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

You are given matrix with n rows and n columns filled with zeroes. You should put k ones in it in such a way that the resulting matrix is symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (the diagonal that goes from the top left to the bottom right corner) and is lexicographically maximal.

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

If there exists no such matrix then output -1.

-----Output-----

1. We have an n×n matrix filled with zeros initially
2. We need to place k ones such that:
   - The matrix is symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if cell (i,j) has a 1, then cell (j,i) must also have a 1)
   - The resulting matrix is lexicographically maximal
3. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every cell (i,j) where i ≠ j, if we place a 1, we must place a 1 at (j,i) as well. This means each off-diagonal pair consumes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal constraint**: For cells on the diagonal (i,i), placing a 1 consumes only 1 one.

3. **Lexicographically maximal**: To maximize lexicographically, we should try to place ones as early as possible in the rows (from top to bottom) and within each row from left to right.

4. **Feasibility**: We need to check if k ones can be placed given the symmetry constraint.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is possible
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix of zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    remaining = k
    
    # Iterate through all positions in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            # Diagonal element
            if i == j:
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            # Upper triangle (i < j)
            elif i < j:
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # Since k <= n*n, remaining should be 0
    # If not, it means we couldn't place all ones, but our logic should ensure it's possible
    # So we just output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

View Extracted Code 
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is possible
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix of zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    remaining = k
    
    # Iterate through all positions in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            # Diagonal element
            if i == j:
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            # Upper triangle (i < j)
            elif i < j:
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # Since k <= n*n, remaining should be 0
    # If not, it means we couldn't place all ones, but our logic should ensure it's possible
    # So we just output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given matrix with n rows and n columns filled with zeroes. You should put k ones in it in such a way that the resulting matrix is symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (the diagonal that goes from the top left to the bottom right corner) and is lexicographically maximal.

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

If there exists no such matrix then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line consists of two numbers n and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6).


-----Output-----

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones such that:
   - The matrix is symmetric with respect to the main diagonal
   - The resulting matrix is lexicographically maximal
3. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry requirement**: If we place a 1 at position (i, j), we must also place a 1 at position (j, i). This means:
   - For i < j: one "pair" of symmetric positions contributes 2 ones to the total count
   - For i = j: the diagonal position contributes 1 one
   - For i > j: automatically covered by symmetry

2. **Lexicographic maximality**: To get the lexicographically largest matrix, we should place ones as early as possible in the rows (from top to bottom) and within each row as early as possible in the columns (from left to right).

3. **Feasibility constraint**: 
   - Each off-diagonal pair contributes 2 ones
   - Each diagonal position contributes 1 one
   - We need exactly k ones

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if possible
max_ones = n * n
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # We need k = d + 2p where 0 <= d <= n and 0 <= p <= n*(n-1)//2
    # d = k - 2p, so we need:
    # 0 <= k - 2p <= n  =>  (k-n) <= 2p <= k  =>  max(0, (k-n)//2) <= p <= min(k//2, n*(n-1)//2)

    min_pairs = max(0, (k - n) // 2)
    
    if min_pairs > max_pairs:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # We can always find a valid p in [min_pairs, max_pairs]
        # For lexicographic maximum, we place as early as possible in row-major order
        # We go through positions where i <= j in row-major order
        matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
        remaining = k
        
        # Go through all positions with i <= j in row-major order
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i, n):
                if i == j:  # diagonal
                    if remaining >= 1:
                        matrix[i][j] = 1
                        remaining -= 1
                    # if remaining >= 2, we might place off-diagonal, but we go in order

                    if remaining >= 2:
                        matrix[i][j] = 1
                        matrix[j][i] = 1
                        remaining -= 2
                    # if remaining < 2, we skip this position (and it's okay because we go in order)
                    
        if remaining > 0:
            print(-1)
        else:
            for row in matrix:
                print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

```python
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
```

View Extracted Code 
n, k = map(int, input().split())

if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Go through positions where i <= j in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if i == j:  # diagonal
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            else:  # i < j, off-diagonal
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For each pair (i,j) where i ≠ j, if we place a 1 at (i,j), we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair takes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal elements**: For diagonal elements (i,i), placing a 1 only requires 1 one (no symmetry constraint needed).

3. **Lexicographically maximal**: To be lexicographically maximal, we want to place ones in the top-leftmost positions possible. This means we should fill the matrix row by row, from left to right, and within each row from left to right.

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k exceeds maximum possible (n*n)
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    remaining = k
    
    # First, place ones on the diagonal in order (top to bottom)
    for i in range(n):
        if remaining > 0:
            matrix[i][i] = 1
            remaining -= 1
    
    # Then, place symmetric pairs in upper triangle (i < j) in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if remaining >= 2:
                matrix[i][j] = 1
                matrix[j][i] = 1
                remaining -= 2
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Our current algorithm places diagonal ones greedily in order, and then pairs in order.

But it doesn't consider that placing a symmetric pair might give a better lexicographic result.

I need to reconsider the lexicographic goal.

For lexicographic maximum, in row-major order, we want to have a 1 as early as possible.

The first positions are (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), ..., (1,0), (1,1), etc.

We have two types of placements:
1. Diagonal placements at (i,i)
2. Off-diagonal placements at (i,j) for i < j, which also affects (j,i)

For lexicographic maximality, we want to place ones at (0,0), then (0,1), then (0,2), etc.

The issue is that (0,1) comes after (0,0) in row-major order.

But (0,1) and (1,0) are symmetric.

If we place (0,1) and (1,0), we get at (0,1)=1, which is earlier than placing a diagonal at (1,1) or (2,2).

In fact, (0,1) appears before (1,1) in row-major order.

So to maximize lexicographic order, we should prioritize off-diagonal pairs that appear early in row-major order.

But we still have the diagonal at (0,0), which is the very first position.

The order of positions in row-major is:
1. (0,0)
2. (0,1)
3. (1,0)
4. (1,1)
5. (0,2)
6. (1,2)
7. (2,0)
8. (2,1)
9. (2,2)

So the first position is (0,0), then (0,1), then (1,0), then (1,1), etc.

Therefore, to maximize lexicographically:
- We must place a 1 at (0,0) if possible (it's the first position)
- Then (0,1) and (1,0) as a pair
- Then (1,1) etc.

But (1,0) appears after (0,1) in row-major order.

So the correct greedy strategy should be:

1. Try to place ones in the matrix in row-major order, and for each position (i,j):
   - If we can place a 1 and it doesn't violate symmetry, and the remaining ones allow it, then place it
   - But we must maintain symmetry

However, we have to decide whether to place a diagonal or a symmetric pair.

For a position (i,j) where i < j, placing a 1 at (i,j) means placing 1 at (j,i).

For a position (i,i), placing 1 means only one cost.

We want to place 1 as early as possible.

In row-major order, (0,0) comes first, then (0,1), then (1,0), then (1,1), then (0,2), etc.

So the algorithm should be:

- For each position (i,j) in row-major order:
  - If i == j (diagonal): we can place 1, costing 1
  - If i < j (upper triangle): we can place 1, but it costs 2 (because we also need (j,i))
  - If i > j (lower triangle): we skip because we only consider upper triangle
  - If remaining >= cost, place the 1(s) and reduce remaining

But we need to do it in row-major order.

However, for a symmetric pair (i,j) with i < j, placing it means we need 2 ones.

For diagonal (i,i), placing means 1 one.

So in row-major order:

- (0,0): cost 1, if remaining >= 1, place it, remaining -= 1
- (0,1): cost 2, if remaining >= 2, place it (and (1,0)), remaining -= 2
- (0,2): cost 2, if remaining >= 2, place it, remaining -= 2
- (1,0): already covered by (0,1) in the symmetric constraint
- (1,1): cost 1, if remaining >= 1, place it, remaining -= 1
- (1,2): cost 2, if remaining >= 2, place it, remaining -= 2
- etc.

This will give a matrix that is lexicographically maximal because we're filling in row-major order.

Let me verify with n=3, k=4:
- (0,0): remaining=4 >=1, place → remaining=3, matrix[0][0]=1
- (0,1): remaining=3 >=2, place → remaining=1, matrix[0][1]=1, matrix[1][0]=1
- (0,2): remaining=1 <2, skip
- (1,0): already placed
- (1,1): remaining=1 >=1, place → remaining=0, matrix[1][1]=1
- (1,2): remaining=0, skip
- (2,0): already covered
- (2,1): covered
- (2,2): remaining=0, skip

Matrix:
Row 0: [1,1,0]
Row 1: [1,1,0]
Row 2: [0,0,0]

Total ones: 1 (pos00) + 2 (pos01,10) + 1 (pos11) = 4

Lexicographically: this is good.

Is there a better one? 
- If we skip (0,0) and place (0,1) and (1,0) and (1,1): 
  - That would be 4 ones, but row0: [0,1,0] vs [1,1,0] → [1,1,0] is larger.

So it's better.

Another possibility: skip (0,0), place (0,1) and (1,0), and (0,2) and (2,0): that's 4 ones, row0: [0,1,1] vs [1,1,0] → [1,1,0] is larger.

So yes, placing at (0,0) is better.

Therefore, the greedy row-major algorithm works.

But what about the feasibility? We need to ensure we don't exceed k.

In this algorithm, we only place when remaining >= cost.

And since we're going in row-major order, we're respecting the symmetry.

Also, we need to ensure that the total ones we place is exactly k.

The cost for each placement:
- Diagonal: 1
- Upper triangle pair: 2

We place in the order of position in row-major.

This will give us exactly k ones.

And it will be lexicographically maximal because we're filling in the order of positions.

Let me check if there are any missing constraints.

The only potential issue is that for a pair (i,j) with i < j, we are placing both (i,j) and (j,i), but in row-major order, (i,j) comes before (j,i) when i < j.

For example, (0,1) comes before (1,0) in row-major order? 
- (0,1) is at row 0, col 1
- (1,0) is at row 1, col 0
- Row-major: (0,0), (0,1), (1,0), (1,1)

So (0,1) comes before (1,0).

Therefore, when we place (0,1), we automatically place (1,0) as well.

This is correct.

So the algorithm is:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Iterate through positions in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            # Skip if we are in lower triangle (i > j) because we will handle symmetry
            # We only consider positions we can place in: diagonal or upper triangle
            if i == j:
                # Diagonal position - cost 1
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            elif i < j:
                # Upper triangle - cost 2 (for both (i,j) and (j,i))
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
            # For i > j: skip, because we will have placed (j,i) when we were at (j,i) and i < j
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, k=1
- (0,0): remaining=1 >=1 → set matrix[0][0]=1, remaining=0
- (0,1): i=0, j=1, i<j, remaining=0 <2 → skip
- (1,0): i=1>j=0, skip
- (1,1): i=j, remaining=0 <1 → skip
- Output: [[1,0],[0,0]] ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=2
- (0,0): remaining=2 >=1 → set, remaining=1
- (0,1): i<j, remaining=1 <2 → skip
- (0,2): skip
- (1,0): i>j, skip
- (1,1): i=j, remaining=1 >=1 → set, remaining=0
- (1,2): i<j, remaining=0 → skip
- (2,0): skip
- (2,1): skip
- (2,2): skip
- Matrix: [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[0,0,0]] ✓

Example 3: n=2, k=5
- k=5 > 4 → output -1 ✓

Another example: n=3, k=4 → as above, should work.

- No placements, matrix all zeros → correct.

What if k=1, n=1? 
- (0,0): place, remaining=0 → matrix=[[1]] → correct.

What if k=2, n=1?

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:

    remaining = k
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given matrix with n rows and n columns filled with zeroes. You should put k ones in it in such a way that the resulting matrix is symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (the diagonal that goes from the top left to the bottom right corner) and is lexicographically maximal.

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

If there exists no such matrix then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line consists of two numbers n and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6).


-----Output-----

If the answer exists then output resulting matrix. Otherwise output -1.

0 0

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix filled with zeros initially
2. We need to place exactly k ones such that:
   - The matrix is symmetric with respect to the main diagonal
   - The resulting matrix is lexicographically maximal
3. If no such matrix exists, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For a matrix to be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal, if we place a 1 at position (i,j), we must also place a 1 at position (j,i). This means:
   - For i = j (main diagonal): placing a 1 only affects one position
   - For i ≠ j: placing a 1 affects two positions (i,j) and (j,i)

2. **Lexicographically maximal**: To make the matrix lexicographically maximal, we want to place 1s as early as possible in the row-major order. This means we should prioritize filling the upper triangle (including the diagonal) from left to right, top to bottom.

3. **Count of ones needed**: 
   - Each off-diagonal pair (i,j) where i < j contributes 2 ones to the total count
   - Each diagonal position (i,i) contributes 1 one to the total count
   - Total ones = sum of diagonal ones + 2 × sum of off-diagonal ones in upper triangle

## Analysis of Feasibility

- Total ones = x + 2y = k

## Code Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check feasibility: maximum possible ones is n*n
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Fill the matrix in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            if i == j:
                # Diagonal element
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            elif i < j:
                # Upper triangle: place pair (i,j) and (j,i)
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # If we still have remaining ones, it's impossible (shouldn't happen due to k <= n*n)
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Wait, but is there a case where we can't use the remaining ones? 
Since maximum ones is n*n and we have k ≤ n*n, and we're using ones in a way that total ones = diagonal ones + 2 * pair count, and we process greedily from top-left, it should always work.

The total ones used is exactly k, so remaining should be 0.

But we have a check for remaining > 0, which should be unreachable since k ≤ n*n and our construction ensures we use exactly k ones.

Actually, let's think: is there a constraint that we might not be able to place exactly k ones due to the parity or other reasons?

We have:
- diagonal: 0 to n ones
- pairs: 0 to C(n,2) pairs, each using 2 ones
- total = diagonal + 2 * pairs

The maximum is n + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

For any k in [0, n²], we can find diagonal d and pairs p such that d + 2p = k, with 0 ≤ d ≤ n, 0 ≤ p ≤ n*(n-1)/2.

Is this always possible?

For a fixed k, we can choose d in {0,1,2,...,n} such that d ≡ k (mod 2) and d ≤ n.

Since there are n+1 values of d, and they cover all parities from 0 to n, and since n ≥ 0, we can always find such d as long as k ≤ n².

The only case is when k > n², which we already check.

So our solution should be correct.

But let's verify edge cases.

Edge case: n=1, k=0 → should output [[0]]
n=1, k=1 → [[1]]
n=1, k=2 → k > 1*1 → -1

n=2, k=4 → max is 4, so should work.
- (0,0): place 1, rem=3
- (0,1): i<j, need 2, have 3 → place pair, rem=1
- (1,0): already set by pair
- (1,1): i==j, need 1, have 1 → place 1
Result: [[1,1],[1,1]] ✓

n=2, k=3:
- (0,0): place 1, rem=2
- (0,1): place pair, rem=0

This is symmetric? Yes, since (0,1) and (1,0) are both 1.

(0,0)=1, (1,1)=0.

The matrix is symmetric.

And we have used exactly 3 ones.

Lexicographically: [[1,1],[1,0]] vs other possibilities.

Alternative: place diagonal at (0,0),(1,1) → [[1,0],[0,1]] which has only 2 ones.

Another: place pair at (0,1) and diagonal at (0,0) → [[1,1],[1,0]] ✓

Is there a larger lexicographic one? 
[[1,1],[1,0]] vs [[1,0],[0,1]] → first row [1,1] > [1,0] ✓

So it's correct.


Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given matrix with n rows and n columns filled with zeroes. You should put k ones in it in such a way that the resulting matrix is symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (the diagonal that goes from the top left to the bottom right corner) and is lexicographically maximal.

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

If there exists no such matrix then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line consists of two numbers n and k (1 ≤ n ≤ 100, 0 ≤ k ≤ 10^6).


-----Output-----

If the answer exists then output resulting matrix. Otherwise output -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2 1

Output
1 0 
0 0 

Input
3 2

Output
1 0 0 
0 1 0 
0 0 0 

Input
2 5

Output
-1

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix filled with zeros initially
2. We need to place exactly k ones such that:
   - The matrix is symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if matrix[i][j] = 1, then matrix[j][i] = 1)
   - The resulting matrix is lexicographically maximal
3. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every off-diagonal position (i,j) where i ≠ j, if we place a 1 at (i,j), we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair consumes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal positions**: For diagonal positions (i,i), placing a 1 only consumes 1 one (since it's symmetric with itself).

3. **Lexicographic maximality**: To be lexicographically maximal, we should place ones as early as possible in the row order, and within each row, as early as possible in the column order.

4. **Feasibility**: 
   - If k is odd and we place ones only on the diagonal, we can always do it (diagonal positions consume 1 each).
   - If k is even, we can use diagonal or off-diagonal positions.
   - However, if k is very large, we might not have enough positions.

## Position Count Analysis

- We have n diagonal positions (each can hold 1 one)
- For off-diagonal pairs: there are (n*(n-1))/2 pairs, each requiring 2 ones to place symmetrically

So maximum ones we can place is: n (diagonal) + 2 * (n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

Therefore, if k > n², it's impossible → output -1.

For feasibility with k ones:
- We can always place ones on the diagonal: n positions
- For off-diagonal, we have (n*(n-1))/2 pairs, each giving 2 ones

## Strategy for Lexicographic Maximal

To be lexicographically maximal, we should:
1. Fill the first row as much as possible from left to right
2. Then fill the second row, and so on
3. But we must maintain symmetry

Since we want lexicographic maximum, we should prioritize positions in row-major order.

For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j (to avoid double counting due to symmetry):
- If i = j (diagonal): we can place 1 using 1 one
- If i < j (upper triangle): we need 2 ones to place at both (i,j) and (j,i)

So we should:
1. First check if k > n² → return -1
2. For positions in row-major order (i,j) where i ≤ j:
   - Try to place a 1 at (i,j)
   - If it's diagonal (i=j): it consumes 1 one
   - If it's off-diagonal (i<j): it consumes 2 ones
   - Place it if we have enough ones remaining
3. After placing, if we still have remaining ones, we can place more in the same pattern

Actually, to be lexicographically maximal, we should try to set the first 1 in the first row, then the first 1 in the second row, etc.

But due to symmetry, if we set position (i,j) where i < j, we must also set (j,i).

So in row-major order, we should consider:
- For each row i from 0 to n-1:
  - For each column j from 0 to n-1:
    - If we can place a 1 at (i,j) with remaining ones, and (i,j) is not symmetric with a previously placed pair, then place it

But since we want lexicographic maximum, we should place ones greedily from top-left to bottom-right.

## Algorithm

1. Check if k > n*n → return -1
2. Create an n×n matrix filled with zeros
3. For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j:
   - If i == j (diagonal): this consumes 1 one
   - If i < j (off-diagonal): this consumes 2 ones
   - If we have sufficient ones, place the 1 at (i,j) and (j,i) if i < j, or just at (i,j) if i == j
4. After processing, if we've used exactly k ones, we're done. Otherwise, we need to check if we can place more.

We have to place exactly k ones. We can:
- Place on diagonal: each costs 1
- Place on symmetric pairs (i,j) where i < j: each costs 2

Let d = number of diagonal ones
Let p = number of symmetric pairs (off-diagonal)

Then: d + 2p = k

Constraints:

- 0 ≤ p ≤ (n*(n-1))//2

We want lexicographically maximal matrix, so we should maximize the first row, then second, etc.

To achieve lexicographic maximum, we should greedily fill from top-left to bottom-right.

For each (i,j) where i ≤ j:

## Step-by-step Algorithm

1. Check if k > n*n → return -1
2. Initialize matrix with zeros
3. remaining = k
4. For i from 0 to n-1:
   - For j from i to n-1:
     - If i == j (diagonal):
       - If remaining >= 1, place 1 at (i,i), remaining -= 1
     - If i < j (off-diagonal):
       - If remaining >= 2, place 1 at (i,j) and (j,i), remaining -= 2

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is greater than maximum possible ones (n*n)
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Fill the matrix in lexicographic order (row by row, column by column)
    # For positions where i <= j (to avoid double counting due to symmetry)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if i == j:  # diagonal position
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            else:  # off-diagonal position (i < j)
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # We should have used exactly k ones
    # If remaining > 0, it means we couldn't place all ones
    # But since k <= n*n, and we're placing in order, this shouldn't happen
    # However, due to our greedy placement, we might have remaining > 0
    # But we already checked k <= n*n, and our placement should use exactly k
    
    # Actually, we need to ensure we use exactly k ones
    # Our greedy placement might leave remaining > 0 if we can't place due to symmetry
    # But we only skip when we don't have enough ones
    
    # If remaining > 0, it means we couldn't place all k ones, which should not happen
    # since we checked k <= n*n, and we can always place k ones in n*n positions
    # But our placement might not cover all cases - we need to make sure we can actually place k ones
    
    # Actually, the constraint is that we can place at most n + 2*(n*(n-1)//2) = n*n ones
    # And we already have k <= n*n
    
    # But can we always place exactly k ones? Yes, because we have a complete set of positions
    # The issue is that we're filling greedily, but we might not use exactly k if we skip some positions
    
    # Actually, we should just output the matrix - we've placed as many as possible
    # But we need to place exactly k ones.
    
    # Our greedy approach ensures we place in lexicographic order, but we must ensure we use exactly k ones.
    
    # Let me reconsider: if k <= n*n, can we always place exactly k ones?
    # We have positions: 
    # - n diagonal positions (cost 1 each)
    # - (n*(n-1))//2 off-diagonal pairs (cost 2 each)
    
    # We want to place exactly k ones, which means we need to find non-negative integers d and p such that:
    # d + 2p = k, where 0 <= d <= n, 0 <= p <= (n*(n-1))//2
    
    # Since k <= n*n, and the maximum value of d+2p is n + 2*(n*(n-1)//2) = n + n*(n-1) = n*n,
    # we know solutions exist for d+2p=k as long as k <= n*n.
    
    # For any k <= n*n, we can find such d and p.
    
    # But our greedy algorithm might not use the minimal positions to achieve k.
    
    # Actually, our greedy algorithm is correct because:
    # We're placing in row-major order (lexicographic), and we're placing as much as possible to maximize lexicographic order.
    
    # Since we're going row by row, column by column, and placing 1s when possible, we're maximizing the value at each position.
    
    # For the diagonal, placing 1 at (i,i) uses 1 one and increases lexicographic value.
    # For off-diagonal (i,j) with i<j, placing 1s at both (i,j) and (j,i) uses 2 ones and increases lexicographic value.
    
    # So by placing a 1 as soon as possible when we have sufficient ones, we maximize lexicographic order.
    
    # The only issue is if k > n*n, which we already checked.
    
    # Therefore, the matrix we constructed uses exactly k ones if we can find a valid configuration.
    
    # But we must check that we actually used exactly k ones. Our algorithm might leave remaining > 0.
    
    # Let's consider: k=1, n=2
    # We place at (0,0): remaining becomes 0 → correct
    
    # k=2, n=3:
    # (0,0): use 1, remaining=1
    # (0,1): need 2, can't place -> skip
    # (0,2): need 2, can't place -> skip
    # (1,1): need 1, can place → use 1 at (1,1), remaining=0
    # Total used: 2 → correct
    
    # k=3, n=3:
    # (0,0): use 1, remaining=2
    # (0,1): use 2, remaining=0, place at (0,1) and (1,0)
    # Total used: 3 → correct
    
    # So our algorithm works.
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal
4. The matrix should be lexicographically maximal
5. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: If we place a 1 at position (i,j), we must also place a 1 at position (j,i). This means:
   - For i = j (main diagonal): each 1 counts as 1
   - For i ≠ j: each 1 pair (i,j) and (j,i) counts as 2 ones

2. **Lexicographically maximal**: To maximize lexicographically, we want to place ones as early as possible in the matrix (top-left to bottom-right order).

3. **Feasibility check**: 
   - The minimum number of ones needed to achieve symmetry is when we place ones on the diagonal
   - The maximum number of ones we can place is limited by the symmetry constraint
   
4. **Strategy**:
   - For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j, we decide how many ones to place
   - We prioritize top-left positions to maximize lexicographic order
   - We need to ensure the total count of ones equals k

## Case Analysis

Let's think about the placement:

- For positions on the diagonal (i,i): each one contributes 1 to the total count
- For off-diagonal pairs (i,j) where i < j: each pair contributes 2 ones to the total count

So if we place:
- d ones on the diagonal
- p pairs of off-diagonal ones (where each pair is (i,j) and (j,i) with i < j)

Then total ones = d + 2p

We need: d + 2p = k

Constraints:
- d ≤ n (at most n diagonal positions)
- p ≥ 0 (non-negative pairs)
- For each pair (i,j) with i < j, we need i < j

To maximize lexicographically, we should:
1. Fill positions (i,j) with i ≤ j in row-major order
2. For each position, if we can place a 1 there without exceeding k, place it

But we need to maintain symmetry.

Actually, we can do:
- We know the total ones needed is k
- We'll iterate through the upper triangular part (including diagonal) in row-major order
- For each position (i,j) with i ≤ j, we decide how many ones to place at (i,j) and (j,i)
- But if i < j, placing a 1 at (i,j) also places at (j,i), so it contributes 2 to the total
- If i = j, it contributes 1

We want lexicographic maximality, so we should place ones as early as possible.

Algorithm:
1. If k > n*n: return -1
2. Initialize matrix with zeros
3. Initialize remaining_ones = k
4. For i from 0 to n-1:
   For j from i to n-1:
     - If i == j: placing a 1 at (i,i) contributes 1 to total
     - If i < j: placing a 1 at (i,j) contributes 2 to total (since (j,i) is also filled)
     - If remaining_ones >= contribution, place it and subtract contribution
     - Else, skip
5. After processing all positions, if remaining_ones > 0, return -1 (shouldn't happen due to our k check)

Wait, is that correct? We might not be able to use all remaining ones, but due to our earlier analysis, we should be able to use exactly k ones.

Actually, since we're processing in lexicographic order and we can place ones optimally, this should work.

But let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, k=1
- i=0, j=0: i=j, contributes 1. remaining_ones=1 ≥ 1, so place one at (0,0)
- i=0, j=1: i<j, contributes 2. remaining_ones=0 < 2, skip
- i=1, j=1: i=j, contributes 1. remaining_ones=0 < 1, skip
Result: [[1,0],[0,0]] ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=2
- We need to place exactly 2 ones due to symmetry.
- i=0, j=0: contributes 1. remaining_ones=2 ≥ 1, place it. remaining_ones=1
- i=0, j=1: contributes 2. remaining_ones=1 < 2, skip
- i=0, j=2: contributes 2. remaining_ones=1 < 2, skip
- i=1, j=1: contributes 1. remaining_ones=1 ≥ 1, place it. remaining_ones=0
- i=1, j=2: contributes 2. remaining_ones=0 < 2, skip
- i=2, j=2: contributes 1. remaining_ones=0 < 1, skip
Result: [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[0,0,0]] ✓

Example 3: n=2, k=5
- n² = 4, k=5 > 4, return -1 ✓

This matches all examples.

One more check: could we place off-diagonal ones earlier? Yes, but in lexicographic order, we process positions in order of (i,j), so (0,0) comes before (0,1), so we handle the diagonal first to maximize lexicographic value.

Wait, actually is (0,0) lexicographically before (0,1)? Yes. So we want to place ones at the earliest positions first.

Therefore, the algorithm is correct.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if it's possible
max_ones = n * n
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining_ones = k
    
    # Fill the matrix in lexicographic order (upper triangular part including diagonal)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if i == j:
                # Diagonal element: contributes 1 to total
                if remaining_ones >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1  # Symmetric, but (j,i) is same as (i,j) when i=j
                    remaining_ones -= 1
            else:
                # Off-diagonal pair: placing 1 at (i,j) and (j,i) contributes 2
                if remaining_ones >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining_ones -= 2
    
    # After processing, if we still have remaining ones, it's impossible
    # But due to our constraint k <= n*n, it should be exactly zero
    if remaining_ones > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

Input

1
1 2
50


Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2
9 11
50


Output

0.833333333333333

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking integer values from [Li, Ri] with equal probability
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have first digit equal to 1
3. The first digit is the most significant digit (MSD)

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, I need to calculate the probability that its value starts with digit 1
2. This is a binomial probability problem: we have N independent trials, each with success probability p_i (probability that value starts with 1), and we want the probability that at least K% of them succeed
3. For a number to start with digit 1, it must be in the range [1, 1.999...], [10, 19.999...], [100, 199.999...], etc.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability that a value starts with digit 1

For a value in range [L, R], I need to count how many numbers start with digit 1.

A number starts with digit 1 if it's in one of these ranges:
- [1, 1.999...]
- [10, 19.999...]
- [100, 199.999...]
- [1000, 1999.999...]
- etc.

For any range [L, R], I need to find the intersection with these ranges.

### Step 2: For each range [10^k, 10^(k+1) - 1], count the numbers that fall in [L, R]

For a given power of 10, the numbers starting with 1 are in [10^k, 2×10^k - 1].

### Step 3: Calculate binomial probability

After getting the success probability for each variable, I need to calculate the probability that at least K% of N variables have first digit 1.

This is a binomial distribution: P(at least K% of N variables succeed) = P(at least floor(N×K/100) variables succeed)

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def count_numbers_starting_with_1(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that start with digit 1"""
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    # For each power of 10, check if there's overlap with [10^k, 2*10^k - 1]
    while power <= r:
        low_bound = power
        high_bound = min(2 * power - 1, r)
        
        # Only consider if this range intersects with [l, r]
        if low_bound <= r:
            start = max(l, low_bound)
            end = min(r, high_bound)
            
            if start <= end:
                count += end - start + 1
                
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def prob_starting_with_1(l, r):
    """Probability that a random number in [l, r] starts with digit 1"""
    count = count_numbers_starting_with_1(l, r)
    total = r - l + 1
    return count / total if total > 0 else 0

def binomial_probability(n, p, k):
    """Calculate binomial probability of at least k successes out of n trials with success probability p"""
    from math import comb
    
    # We need at least k successes
    # But k is given as percentage, so we need at least (k/100)*n successes
    required_min = (k * n) // 100
    required_min = max(required_min, 0)
    
    # If we need at least required_min successes
    prob = 0.0
    for i in range(required_min, n + 1):
        # Probability of exactly i successes
        prob_i = comb(n, i) * (p ** i) * ((1 - p) ** (n - i))
        prob += prob_i
    
    return prob

# Read input
n = int(input())
variables = []
for _ in range(n):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())
    variables.append((l, r))

k = int(input())

# Calculate success probability for each variable
p_success = []
for l, r in variables:
    prob = prob_starting_with_1(l, r)
    p_success.append(prob)

# Calculate the overall probability using binomial distribution
# We need at least K% of the values to start with 1
required_min = (k * n) // 100
required_min = max(required_min, 0)

# Since we have independent variables with different success probabilities,
# we need to use the probability generating function or inclusion-exclusion
# But actually, since the variables are independent, we can use the multinomial approach

# For independent variables with different success probabilities, 
# we need to sum over all combinations where at least required_min variables have success

# However, n <= 1000, but we can't do a full sum over all possibilities due to size.
# Instead, we need to use dynamic programming or approximation.

# Actually, the standard approach for independent trials with different success probabilities is to use DP.

# Let's use DP where dp[i][j] = probability that among first i variables, exactly j have first digit 1
# Then answer is sum of dp[n][j] for j >= required_min

# But note: we can compute this with DP since n <= 1000

# Initialize DP table
# dp[i][j] = probability that first i variables have exactly j successes
dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
dp[0] = 1.0

for i in range(n):
    l, r = variables[i]
    p = prob_starting_with_1(l, r)
    new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    
    for j in range(n + 1):
        if dp[j] == 0:
            continue
        # Don't have success
        new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
        # Have success
        if j + 1 <= n:
            new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
    
    dp = new_dp

# Answer is sum of probabilities for j >= required_min
result = 0.0
for j in range(required_min, n + 1):
    result += dp[j]

print(f"{result:.15f}")
```

def count_numbers_starting_with_1(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that start with digit 1"""
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    # For each power of 10, check if there's overlap with [10^k, 2*10^k - 1]
    while power <= r:
        low_bound = power
        high_bound = min(2 * power - 1, r)
        
        # Only consider if this range intersects with [l, r]
        if low_bound <= r:
            start = max(l, low_bound)
            end = min(r, high_bound)
            
            if start <= end:
                count += end - start + 1
                
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def prob_starting_with_1(l, r):
    """Probability that a random number in [l, r] starts with digit 1"""
    count = count_numbers_starting_with_1(l, r)
    total = r - l + 1
    return count / total if total > 0 else 0

def binomial_probability(n, p, k):
    """Calculate binomial probability of at least k successes out of n trials with success probability p"""
    from math import comb
    
    # We need at least k successes
    # But k is given as percentage, so we need at least (k/100)*n successes
    required_min = (k * n) // 100
    required_min = max(required_min, 0)
    
    # If we need at least required_min successes
    prob = 0.0
    for i in range(required_min, n + 1):
        # Probability of exactly i successes
        prob_i = comb(n, i) * (p ** i) * ((1 - p) ** (n - i))
        prob += prob_i
    
    return prob

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking values in the range [Li, Ri] uniformly
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have their first digit equal to 1
3. The first digit (most significant digit) of a number is found by repeatedly dividing by 10 until we get a single digit

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value has first digit 1
2. The first digit is 1 when the value is in the range [1, 1.999...], [10, 19.999...], [100, 199.999...], etc.
3. For any number x, the first digit is 1 if x ∈ [10^k, 2×10^k) for some k ≥ 0

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1

For a variable with range [L, R], we need to find the length of the union of all intervals in [10^k, 2×10^k) that intersect with [L, R].

### Step 2: Use dynamic programming or inclusion-exclusion

Since we need the probability that at least K% of variables have first digit 1, we can:
- Calculate the probability p_i for each variable i to have first digit 1
- Then find the probability that at least ⌈N×K/100⌉ variables have first digit 1

### Step 3: Calculate first digit distribution

For a given range [L, R], the values that have first digit 1 are those in the union of intervals [10^k, 2×10^k) ∩ [L, R] for k ≥ 0.

### Step 4: Implementation approach

1. For each variable, compute the probability that its value has first digit 1
2. Use dynamic programming to compute the probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
3. Sum probabilities for j ≥ ceil(N×K/100)

```python
def first_digit_is_one(x):
    """Check if the first digit of x is 1"""
    if x == 0:
        return False
    x = abs(x)
    while x >= 10:
        x //= 10
    return x == 1

def get_prob_first_digit_one(l, r):
    """Get probability that a random variable in [l, r] has first digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0.0
    
    total_length = r - l + 1
    if total_length == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    # Count numbers in [l, r] that have first digit 1
    count = 0
    
    # Check each power of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        start = power
        end = min(2 * power - 1, r)
        
        # Find intersection with [l, r]
        left = max(start, l)
        right = min(end, r)
        
        if left <= right:
            count += right - left + 1
        
        power *= 10
    
    return count / total_length

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1
    probs = []
    for l, r in variables:
        prob = get_prob_first_digit_one(l, r)
        probs.append(prob)
    
    # Calculate minimum number of variables needed to have first digit 1
    threshold = (k * n + 50) // 100  # ceil(k*n/100)
    
    # Use DP to calculate probability that at least 'threshold' variables have first digit 1
    # dp[i][j] = probability that exactly j variables out of first i have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = probs[i]
        
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Case 1: current variable does NOT have first digit 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1.0 - p)
                # Case 2: current variable DOES have first digit 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all j >= threshold
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(threshold, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

View Extracted Code 
def get_prob_first_digit_one(l, r):
    """Get probability that a random variable in [l, r] has first digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0.0
    
    total_length = r - l + 1
    if total_length == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    count = 0
    
    # Check each power of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        start = power
        end = min(2 * power - 1, r)
        
        # Find intersection with [l, r]
        left = max(start, l)
        right = min(end, r)
        
        if left <= right:
            count += right - left + 1
        
        power *= 10
    
    return count / total_length

n = int(input())
variables = []
for _ in range(n):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())
    variables.append((l, r))

k = int(input())

# Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1
probs = []
for l, r in variables:
    prob = get_prob_first_digit_one(l, r)
    probs.append(prob)

# Calculate minimum number of variables needed to have first digit 1
threshold = (k * n + 99) // 100  # ceil(k*n/100)

# Use DP to calculate probability that at least 'threshold' variables have first digit 1
# dp[i][j] = probability that exactly j variables out of first i have first digit 1
dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
dp[0] = 1.0

for i in range(n):
    new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    p = probs[i]
    
    for j in range(n + 1):
        if dp[j] > 0:
            # Case 1: current variable does NOT have first digit 1
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1.0 - p)
            # Case 2: current variable DOES have first digit 1
            if j + 1 <= n:
                new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
    
    dp = new_dp

# Sum probabilities for all j >= threshold
result = 0.0
for j in range(threshold, n + 1):
    result += dp[j]

print(f"{result:.15f}")
