Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

Input

1
1 2
50


Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2
9 11
50


Output

0.833333333333333

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking values from interval [Li, Ri] with equal probability
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have first digit equal to 1
3. "First digit" means the most significant digit (MSD) - for example, 123 has MSD 1, 456 has MSD 4

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value has first digit 1
2. The first digit is 1 for numbers in ranges: [1, 1), [10, 20), [100, 120), [1000, 1200), etc.
3. More precisely, numbers have first digit 1 if they are in [10^k, 2*10^k) for k = 0, 1, 2, ...

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1

For a variable with range [L, R], we need to:
- Find all intervals of the form [10^k, 2*10^k) that intersect with [L, R]
- Calculate the total length of intersection points
- Divide by (R - L + 1) to get the probability

### Step 2: Use dynamic programming or direct probability calculation

Since we need at least K% of variables to have first digit 1, we can:
- Calculate the probability for each variable independently
- Use the binomial probability formula to find the probability that at least floor(N*K/100) variables have first digit 1

### Step 3: Implementation details

For each range [L, R], we need to find all intervals where first digit is 1:
- For each power of 10 k: [10^k, 2*10^k)
- Find intersection with [L, R]
- Sum up the intersection lengths

```python
def count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r):
    """Calculate the number of integers in [l, r] that have first digit 1"""
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    # We only need to check powers of 10 up to log10(r)
    while power <= r:
        low_bound = power
        high_bound = min(2 * power, r + 1)
        
        # Find intersection of [l, r] and [low_bound, high_bound)
        left = max(l, low_bound)
        right = min(r, high_bound - 1)
        
        if left <= right:
            count += right - left + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    ranges = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        ranges.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in ranges:
        total_count = r - l + 1
        first_digit_1_count = count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r)
        p = first_digit_1_count / total_count
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # We need at least k% of variables to have first digit 1
    # That means at least (k * n / 100) variables
    
    min_required = (k * n + 99) // 100  # Ceiling of k*n/100
    
    # If no variable can have first digit 1, or if k is 0, handle accordingly
    if min_required == 0:
        # At least 0% is always true, so probability is 1
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # If we need at least min_required variables to have first digit 1
    # We use the fact that the events are independent
    
    # We can use dynamic programming or binomial distribution
    # But since n <= 1000, we need to be careful with efficiency
    
    # Actually, we need to sum over all possible combinations where at least min_required have first digit 1
    # This is a binomial-like distribution
    
    from math import comb, log10
    
    # Calculate probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
    prob = 0.0
    
    # We need at least min_required variables
    required = min_required
    
    # Use DP to avoid calculating all combinations
    # dp[i][j] = probability that exactly j variables among first i have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = p_values[i]
        q = 1.0 - p
        
        # For each possible count, update the new state
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Don't choose variable i
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * q
                # Choose variable i
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum all probabilities where count >= required
    for j in range(required, n + 1):
        prob += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{prob:.15f}")

solve()
```

```python
def count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have first digit 1"""
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    # For each power of 10, check interval [10^k, 2*10^k)
    while power <= r:
        # Current interval where first digit is 1: [power, min(2*power, r+1))
        start = power
        end = min(2 * power, r + 1)
        
        # Intersection with [l, r]
        left = max(l, start)
        right = min(r, end - 1)
        
        if left <= right:
            count += right - left + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count

n = int(input())
ranges = []
for _ in range(n):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1

for l, r in ranges:
    total_count = r - l + 1
    first_digit_1_count = count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r)
    p = first_digit_1_count / total_count
    p_values.append(p)

# We need at least k% of variables to have first digit 1
# That means at least (k * n / 100) variables
min_required = (k * n + 99) // 100  # Ceiling of k*n/100

# If no variables needed, probability is 1
if min_required == 0:
    print("1.000000000000000")
else:
    # Use dynamic programming to calculate probability
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for p in p_values:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Don't select this variable
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
                # Select this variable
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all states with at least min_required variables
    result = sum(dp[j] for j in range(min_required, n + 1))
    print(f"{result:.15f}")
```

    count = 0

        # Current interval where first digit is 1: [power, min(2*power, r+1))
        start = power
        end = min(2 * power, r + 1)

        left = max(l, start)
        right = min(r, end - 1)
        
        if left <= right:
            count += right - left + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have N random variables, each taking integer values from [Li, Ri] with equal probability. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have their most significant digit (MSD) equal to 1.

Key points:
1. Each value is chosen uniformly from its interval
2. We care about the first digit (MSD) of each value
3. We want the probability that at least K% of the values have MSD = 1
4. Answer should have precision of at least 10^-9

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understanding MSD = 1

A number has MSD = 1 if it falls in intervals like:
- [1, 1] 
- [10, 19]
- [100, 199]
- [1000, 1999]
- etc.

In general, a number x has MSD = 1 if it belongs to one of the intervals [10^k, 2*10^k - 1] for k = 0, 1, 2, ...

### Step 2: For Each Variable, Calculate Probability of MSD = 1

For each random variable with range [Li, Ri], we need to:
1. Find all intervals of the form [10^k, 2*10^k - 1] that overlap with [Li, Ri]
2. Calculate the total length of intersection between [Li, Ri] and all these intervals
3. Divide by (Ri - Li + 1) to get the probability

### Step 3: Probability of Exactly t Values Having MSD = 1

This follows a binomial distribution. For each variable, we have a probability p_i of having MSD = 1.

We need to calculate the probability that at least K% of the N values have MSD = 1.

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def get_msd_1_probability(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random variable in [l, r] has MSD = 1"""
    if r < 1:
        return 0.0
    
    total_length = r - l + 1
    if total_length == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    # We need to find all intervals of form [10^k, 2*10^k - 1] that overlap with [l, r]
    prob = 0.0
    
    # Start from k = 0 and go up while 10^k <= r
    k = 0
    while True:
        lower_bound = 10 ** k
        upper_bound = min(2 * 10 ** k - 1, r)
        
        # Check if this interval overlaps with [l, r]
        if lower_bound > r:
            break
            
        # Find intersection of [lower_bound, upper_bound] with [l, r]
        start = max(l, lower_bound)
        end = min(r, upper_bound)
        
        if start <= end:
            intersection_length = end - start + 1
            prob += intersection_length
    
    return prob / total_length

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability that each variable has MSD = 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in variables:
        p = get_msd_1_probability(l, r)
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # We need probability that at least K% of variables have MSD = 1
    # That means at least ceil(K * n / 100) variables have MSD = 1
    min_count = (k * n + 100) // 100  # ceil(k * n / 100)
    
    # If K >= 100, we need at least 100% = n variables
    if k >= 100:
        min_count = n
    
    # Calculate probability using dynamic programming
    # dp[i][j] = probability that first i variables have j of them with MSD = 1
    # We need at least min_count variables
    
    # Since n <= 1000, we can use DP with state (variable_index, count)
    # But count can be up to n, so we need to optimize
    
    # We'll use DP where dp[j] = probability of having j variables with MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # Initially, 0 variables have MSD = 1 with probability 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = p_values[i]
        q = 1.0 - p
        
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Next variable has MSD = 1 with probability p
                new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
                # Next variable doesn't have MSD = 1 with probability q
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * q
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= min_count
    result = 0.0
    for count in range(min_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[count]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

For K%, we need at least K/100 * N variables to have MSD = 1.

```python
def get_msd_1_probability(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random variable in [l, r] has MSD = 1"""
    if r < 1:
        return 0.0
    
    total_length = r - l + 1
    if total_length == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    prob = 0.0
    
    # We need to find all intervals of form [10^k, 2*10^k - 1] that overlap with [l, r]
    k = 0
    while True:
        lower_bound = 10 ** k
        upper_bound = min(2 * 10 ** k - 1, r)
        
        # Check if this interval overlaps with [l, r]
        if lower_bound > r:
            break
            
        # Find intersection of [lower_bound, upper_bound] with [l, r]
        start = max(l, lower_bound)
        end = min(r, upper_bound)
        
        if start <= end:
            intersection_length = end - start + 1
            prob += intersection_length
    
    return prob / total_length

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability that each variable has MSD = 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in variables:
        p = get_msd_1_probability(l, r)
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # Calculate minimum number of variables needed to have at least K%
    min_count = (k * n + 99) // 100  # ceil(k * n / 100)
    
    # DP to calculate probability of exactly j variables having MSD = 1
    # dp[j] = probability that j variables have MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for prob in p_values:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # With probability prob, current variable has MSD = 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * prob
                # With probability (1 - prob), current variable doesn't have MSD = 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - prob)
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= min_count
    result = 0.0
    for count in range(min_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[count]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")
```

def get_msd_1_probability(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random variable in [l, r] has MSD = 1"""
    if r < 1:
        return 0.0
    
    total_length = r - l + 1
    if total_length == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    prob = 0.0
    
    # We need to find all intervals of form [10^k, 2*10^k - 1] that overlap with [l, r]
    k = 0
    while True:
        lower_bound = 10 ** k
        upper_bound = min(2 * 10 ** k - 1, r)
        
        # Check if this interval overlaps with [l, r]
        if lower_bound > r:
            break
            
        # Find intersection of [lower_bound, upper_bound] with [l, r]
        start = max(l, lower_bound)
        end = min(r, upper_bound)
        
        if start <= end:
            intersection_length = end - start + 1
            prob += intersection_length
    
    return prob / total_length

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability that each variable has MSD = 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in variables:
        p = get_msd_1_probability(l, r)
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # Calculate minimum number of variables needed to have at least K%
    min_count = (k * n + 99) // 100  # ceil(k * n / 100)
    
    # DP to calculate probability of exactly j variables having MSD = 1
    # dp[j] = probability that j variables have MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for prob in p_values:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # With probability prob, current variable has MSD = 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * prob
                # With probability (1 - prob), current variable doesn't have MSD = 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - prob)
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= min_count
    result = 0.0
    for count in range(min_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[count]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

1

Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2

50


Output

0.833333333333333

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking values from [Li, Ri] uniformly
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of the values have their first digit equal to 1
3. The first digit (most significant digit) of a number is the leftmost digit when written in decimal

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value has first digit 1
2. This is a binomial probability problem: we have N independent Bernoulli trials (each variable either has first digit 1 or not)
3. We need the probability that at least K% of the N values have first digit 1
4. K% of N means at least (K * N / 100) values need to have first digit 1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability that a single value has first digit 1

For a number in range [L, R], we need to count how many numbers have first digit 1.

A number has first digit 1 if it's in one of these ranges:
- [1, 9]
- [10, 19]
- [100, 199]
- [1000, 1999]
- and so on...

For a given range [L, R], we need to find all numbers that start with 1.

### Step 2: Count numbers with first digit 1 in [L, R]

To find numbers with first digit 1:
1. Find the smallest power of 10 ≥ L
2. Find the largest power of 10 ≤ R
3. For each interval [10^k, 10^(k+1) - 1], count how many numbers in [L, R] fall in that range

### Step 3: Apply binomial probability

For each random variable, calculate p = probability(first digit is 1)
Then the probability that exactly m out of n variables have first digit 1 is binomial probability:
P(m) = C(n, m) * p^m * (1-p)^(n-m)

We need P(m) for all m ≥ ceil(K*N/100)

### Step 4: Implementation details

I need to be careful with the first digit calculation. For a number x, the first digit is 1 if:
- 10^k ≤ x < 2*10^k for some integer k

```python
import math
from decimal import Decimal, getcontext

# Set precision high enough for the required accuracy
getcontext().prec = 50

def first_digit_is_1(x):
    """Check if the first digit of x is 1"""
    if x == 0:
        return False
    return (x >= 1) and (x < 2 * (x // 10))

def count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have first digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0
    
    count = 0
    # Find the power of 10 range where first digit is 1
    # Numbers with first digit 1 are in intervals [10^k, 2*10^k) for k = 0, 1, 2, ...
    
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        # Current interval is [10^k, 2*10^k)
        low = power
        high = min(2 * power - 1, r)
        
        # Find overlap with [l, r]
        start = max(l, low)
        end = min(r, high)
        
        if start <= end:
            count += end - start + 1
        
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def probability_first_digit_1(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random number from [l, r] has first digit 1"""
    total_count = r - l + 1
    if total_count == 0:
        return 0.0
    
    count_1 = count_first_digit_1_in_range(l, r)
    return count_1 / total_count

def binomial_coefficient(n, k):
    """Calculate C(n, k)"""
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    if k == 0 or k == n:
        return 1
    
    k = min(k, n - k)
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    return result

def binomial_probability(n, p, m):
    """Calculate probability of exactly m successes in n trials with success probability p"""
    if m < 0 or m > n:
        return 0
    
    # Calculate C(n, m) * p^m * (1-p)^(n-m)
    c = binomial_coefficient(n, m)
    prob_m = c * (p ** m) * ((1 - p) ** (n - m))
    return prob_m

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to have first digit 1
    p_list = []
    for l, r in variables:
        p = probability_first_digit_1(l, r)
        p_list.append(p)
    
    # Find minimum number of variables that need to have first digit 1
    min_needed = (k * n + 100) // 100  # ceil(k*n/100)
    
    # Calculate total probability that at least min_needed variables have first digit 1
    # Since variables are independent, we can use the binomial distribution
    # But we need the probability that at least min_needed variables have first digit 1
    
    # However, this is not exactly binomial because each variable has different probability
    # We need to use the inclusion of probabilities from each variable
    
    # Actually, we can compute the probability using dynamic programming
    # Let dp[i][j] = probability that after considering first i variables, exactly j have first digit 1
    
    # Since n <= 1000, we can do DP with O(n^2)
    
    # Initialize DP
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # Initially 0 variables with first digit 1
    
    # Process each variable
    for i in range(n):
        l, r = variables[i]
        p = probability_first_digit_1(l, r)
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] == 0:
                continue
            # Current variable doesn't have first digit 1
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
            # Current variable has first digit 1
            if j + 1 <= n:
                new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all states >= min_needed
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(min_needed, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking integer values from [Li, Ri] with equal probability
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have 1 as their first digit (most significant digit)
3. For a number to have 1 as its first digit, it must be in the range [1, 1.999...], [10, 19.999...], [100, 199.999...], etc.

## Key Insights

1. First digit of a number can be determined by looking at its logarithm: if log10(n) = a + b where a is integer and b is fractional, then the first digit is floor(10^b)
2. For first digit to be 1, we need b ∈ [0, log10(2)) where log10(2) ≈ 0.3010
3. So numbers in ranges [10^k, 10^k × 2) have first digit 1 for any k
4. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value falls in any interval of the form [10^k, 10^k × 2)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. For each interval [Li, Ri], calculate the probability that a value falls in a range that starts with digit 1
2. This happens when the value is in [10^k, 10^k × 2) for some k
3. For each k, find the intersection of [Li, Ri] with [10^k, 10^k × 2)
4. The probability for this intersection is (length of intersection) / (Ri - Li + 1)
5. The total probability that a value has first digit 1 is the sum of probabilities for all valid k

6. Since we need at least K% of values to have first digit 1, this becomes a binomial probability problem
7. For each possible count of values with first digit 1, calculate the probability and sum those where count ≥ K% of N

## Implementation

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def get_first_digit_prob(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random value from [l, r] has first digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0.0
    
    prob = 0.0
    # Check all possible ranges [10^k, 10^k * 2) for which [10^k, 10^k * 2) overlaps with [l, r]
    
    # We only need to consider k such that 10^k <= r and 10^k * 2 >= l
    # k ranges from -floor(log10(l)) to floor(log10(r))
    
    # Start from a reasonable lower bound
    k = -100  # safe lower bound (since values up to 10^18)
    while True:
        low_k = 10 ** k
        high_k = low_k * 2
        
        # Find intersection of [l, r] and [low_k, high_k]
        start = max(l, low_k)
        end = min(r, high_k)
        
        if start >= end:
            # No intersection
            k += 1
            if k > 100:  # safe upper bound
                break
            continue
            
        # Add probability of this intersection
        intersection_length = end - start + 1
        total_length = r - l + 1
        prob += intersection_length / total_length
        
        # Check if we need to continue to next k
        k += 1
        if k > 100:  # safe upper bound
            break
    
    return prob

def main():
    n = int(input())
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        intervals.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # For each variable, calculate probability that it has first digit 1
    p1 = []
    for l, r in intervals:
        prob = get_first_digit_prob(l, r)
        p1.append(prob)
    
    # Now we need probability that at least K% of the n values have first digit 1
    # That is, at least floor(K * n / 100) values have first digit 1
    
    required_count = (k * n + 50) // 100  # ceil(K * n / 100)
    
    # If required_count is 0, then we need at least 0 values, which is always true
    if required_count == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # Use dynamic programming to calculate the probability that exactly j values have first digit 1
    # dp[i][j] = probability that after considering first i variables, exactly j have first digit 1
    
    # We'll use a 1D DP array and update it iteratively
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j values have first digit 1
    
    # Initialize: 0 values have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = p1[i]
        
        # For each possible current count j
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] == 0.0:
                continue
                
            # Value i has first digit 1 with probability p
            # Value i doesn't have first digit 1 with probability (1-p)
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)  # doesn't have first digit 1
            new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p   # has first digit 1
            
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= required_count
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(required_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the first digit calculation. Let me fix this:

```python
import math

def get_first_digit_prob(l, r):
    """Calculate probability that a random value from [l, r] has first digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0.0
    
    prob = 0.0
    # We need to find all intervals [10^k, 10^k * 2) that overlap with [l, r]
    
    # Find the range of k values to consider
    # k such that 10^k <= r and 10^k * 2 >= l (or just overlapping)
    
    # Start from lower bound
    k = 0
    while True:
        low_k = 10 ** k
        high_k = low_k * 2
        
        # Find intersection of [l, r] and [low_k, high_k]
        start = max(l, low_k)
        end = min(r, high_k)
        
        if start >= end:
            # No intersection
            k += 1
            # Edge case: if k is too large, break
            if low_k > r:
                break
            continue
        
        # Add probability of this intersection
        intersection_length = end - start + 1
        total_length = r - l + 1
        prob += intersection_length / total_length
        
        k += 1
        # Avoid infinite loop
        if k > 100:  # 10^100 is way beyond 10^18
            break
    
    # Also need to handle k negative values
    # For negative k, we have ranges like [10^-1, 10^-1 * 2) = [0.1, 0.2)
    # But since our numbers are at least 1, we only need k >= 0
    # Actually, any positive number has first digit >= 1, so we're good
    
    return prob

def main():
    n = int(input())
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        intervals.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # For each variable, calculate probability that it has first digit 1
    p1 = []
    for l, r in intervals:
        prob = get_first_digit_prob(l, r)
        p1.append(prob)
    
    # Required count: at least K% of n values have first digit 1
    required_count = (k * n + 50) // 100  # ceil(K * n / 100)
    
    if required_count == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # DP to calculate probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
    # dp[j] = probability that j variables have first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = p1[i]
        
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] == 0.0:
                continue
                
            # Variable i doesn't have first digit 1
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
            # Variable i has first digit 1
            if j + 1 <= n:
                new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for counts >= required_count
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(required_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

    """Calculate probability that a random value from [l, r] has first digit 1"""

        return 0.0

        start = max(l, low_k)
        end = min(r, high_k)
        
        if start >= end:

            k += 1
            # Edge case: if k is too large, break
            if low_k > r:
                break
            continue

        intersection_length = end - start + 1
        total_length = r - l + 1
        prob += intersection_length / total_length

            break

    # Actually, any positive number has first digit >= 1, so we're good
    
    return prob

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking integer values in range [Li, Ri] with equal probability
2. For each value, we extract the most significant digit (MSD)
3. We need to find the probability that at least K% of the values have MSD = 1
4. This means we need at least K% of N values to have first digit 1

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, I need to calculate the probability that its value has MSD = 1
2. The values with MSD = 1 are: 1, 10-19, 100-199, 1000-1999, etc.
3. For any range [L, R], I need to count how many numbers in this range have MSD = 1
4. The probability for each variable is (count of numbers with MSD = 1) / (range size)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Function to count numbers with MSD = 1 in range [L, R]

For a given range [L, R], I need to count numbers that have first digit 1.
These numbers are in ranges: [1, 1], [10, 19], [100, 199], [1000, 1999], etc.

### Step 2: Calculate probability for each variable

For each variable i, calculate P_i = (count of numbers with MSD = 1 in [Li, Ri]) / (Ri - Li + 1)

### Step 3: Calculate overall probability

We need the probability that at least K% of N values have MSD = 1.

This is equivalent to: P(at least (K/100) * N values have MSD = 1)

Let T = (K/100) * N. We need at least ⌈T⌉ values to have MSD = 1.

This is a binomial probability problem where each variable independently has probability p_i of having MSD = 1.

The overall probability is the sum over j from ⌈T⌉ to N of:
P(exactly j values have MSD = 1) = Σ (over all combinations of j variables that get MSD = 1) of (product of their probabilities) * (product of (1 - probability) for others)

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def count_numbers_with_msd_1(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have most significant digit 1"""
    count = 0
    # Numbers with MSD 1 are in ranges: 1-1, 10-19, 100-199, 1000-1999, etc.
    current = 1
    while current <= r:
        next_range = current * 10
        # The range is [current, min(next_range - 1, r)]
        start = current
        end = min(next_range - 1, r)
        
        # If start > r, this range is beyond our range
        if start > r:
            break
            
        # If start < l, we need to start from max(l, start)
        start = max(start, l)
        
        # If end < start, no overlap
        if end < start:
            current *= 10
            continue
            
        # Add the count in the valid range [start, end]
        count += (end - start + 1)
        
        current *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    target_count = (k * n) // 100
    
    # If target_count is 0, we need at least 0 values with MSD = 1
    # This is always true, so probability is 1
    if target_count == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable
    prob = []
    for l, r in variables:
        count_one = count_numbers_with_msd_1(l, r)
        total_range = r - l + 1
        p = count_one / total_range
        prob.append(p)
    
    # We need at least target_count values to have MSD = 1
    # Use dynamic programming to calculate the probability
    # dp[i][j] = probability that exactly j of first i variables have MSD = 1
    
    # Maximum possible count
    max_j = n
    
    # dp[j] = probability that currently j values have MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (max_j + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # Initially 0 values have MSD = 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (max_j + 1)
        p = prob[i]
        one_prob = p
        not_one_prob = 1.0 - p
        
        # For each possible current count
        for j in range(max_j + 1):
            if dp[j] == 0.0:
                continue
                
            # If we choose the i-th variable to have MSD = 1
            new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * one_prob
            
            # If we choose the i-th variable not to have MSD = 1
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * not_one_prob
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= target_count
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(target_count, max_j + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print("{:.15f}".format(result))

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be careful about the target_count. Since we need at least K%, and K% of N might not be integer, I should use ceiling.

Actually: "at least K% of those values" means we need at least (K * N / 100) values.

But since we can't have fractional values, we need at least ceil(K * N / 100) values.

```python
import sys

def count_numbers_with_msd_1(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have most significant digit 1"""
    if l > r:
        return 0
    
    count = 0
    # Numbers with MSD = 1: 1-1, 10-19, 100-199, 1000-1999, etc.
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        start_of_range = power
        end_of_range = min(power * 10 - 1, r)
        
        # If start_of_range is already beyond r, break
        if start_of_range > r:
            break
            
        # Find intersection with [l, r]
        low = max(l, start_of_range)
        high = min(end_of_range, r)
        
        if low <= high:
            count += high - low + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate minimum number of values needed to have MSD = 1
    # We need at least k% of n values
    min_count = (k * n + 99) // 100  # This is equivalent to ceil(k*n/100)
    
    # If we need 0 values, probability is 1
    if min_count == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable
    prob = []
    for l, r in variables:
        count_one = count_numbers_with_msd_1(l, r)
        total_range = r - l + 1
        p = count_one / total_range
        prob.append(p)
    
    # DP to calculate probability of having at least min_count variables with MSD = 1
    # dp[i] = probability that exactly i variables have MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for i in range(n):
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        p = prob[i]
        not_p = 1.0 - p
        
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] == 0.0:
                continue
                
            # Current variable has MSD = 1
            if j + 1 <= n:
                new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
                
            # Current variable does NOT have MSD = 1
            new_dp[j] += dp[j] * not_p
        
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= min_count
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(min_count, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print("{:.15f}".format(result))

solve()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

Input

1
1 2
50


Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2
9 11
50


Output

0.833333333333333

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking integer values from [Li, Ri] uniformly
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these variables have a first digit of 1
3. First digit of a number is the most significant digit (MSD)

## Key Insights

1. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value has first digit 1
2. The events are independent, so we can use binomial probability
3. For a number to have first digit 1, it must be in the range [1, 9], [10, 99], [100, 999], etc.
4. More generally, a number has first digit 1 if it's in the interval [10^d, 2×10^d) for some d ≥ 0

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability that a single variable has first digit 1

For a range [L, R], we need to count how many numbers have first digit 1.

A number has first digit 1 if it's in [10^d, 2×10^d) for some d.

For a given range [L, R], we need to find all numbers in this range that fall into any interval [10^d, 2×10^d).

### Step 2: Use binomial probability

If p is the probability that a single variable has first digit 1, then the probability that at least K% of N variables have first digit 1 is:

P(at least k% of N variables have first digit 1) = Σ (from i=⌈k×N/100⌉ to N) of C(N,i) × p^i × (1-p)^(N-i)

### Step 3: Implementation details

1. For each variable, calculate the count of numbers with first digit 1 in its range
2. Calculate the probability p for each variable
3. Use dynamic programming or direct computation to calculate the binomial sum

```python
def count_with_first_digit_1(l, r):
    """Count how many numbers in [l, r] have first digit 1"""
    count = 0
    # Consider all possible powers of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        # Interval [power, 2*power) has first digit 1
        low = max(l, power)
        high = min(r, 2 * power - 1)
        if low <= high:
            count += high - low + 1
        power *= 10
    return count

def combination(n, k):
    """Calculate C(n, k)"""
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    if k == 0 or k == n:
        return 1
    
    # Use smaller of k and n-k for efficiency
    k = min(k, n - k)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    return result

def binomial_probability(n, k, p):
    """Calculate P(exactly k successes in n trials with success probability p)"""
    if k > n or k < 0:
        return 0
    if k == 0:
        return (1 - p) ** n
    if k == n:
        return p ** n
    
    # Calculate C(n,k) * p^k * (1-p)^(n-k)
    comb = combination(n, k)
    prob = comb * (p ** k) * ((1 - p) ** (n - k))
    return prob

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k_percent = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability that each variable has first digit 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in variables:
        count_1 = count_with_first_digit_1(l, r)
        total_count = r - l + 1
        p = count_1 / total_count if total_count > 0 else 0
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # The overall probability is the probability that at least k_percent% of the variables have first digit 1
    # We need at least ceil(k_percent * n / 100) variables to have first digit 1
    min_required = (k_percent * n + 99) // 100  # ceil(k_percent * n / 100)
    
    # Since the variables are independent, we can calculate the probability
    # using the fact that each variable has its own probability p_i of having first digit 1
    # But the events are not identical, so we need to consider the joint distribution
    
    # Actually, we need to calculate the probability that at least min_required variables have first digit 1
    # This requires the distribution of the sum of Bernoulli trials with different success probabilities
    
    # However, this is computationally complex. Let's simplify.
    # The problem likely expects us to assume that all variables have the same probability,
    # or we need to compute the probability more carefully.
    
    # Alternative approach: since the constraints are complex, maybe we're meant to calculate
    # the probability that a randomly selected value has first digit 1 for each variable,
    # and then use binomial distribution with the average probability.
    
    # Actually, let's reconsider. We need the exact probability.
    # We can use dynamic programming to calculate the probability distribution.
    
    # DP state: dp[i][j] = probability that after considering first i variables, exactly j have first digit 1
    # The total number of variables is at most 1000, and we need to track the sum, which can be up to 1000.
    # This should be feasible.
    
    # But note: we don't know the exact distribution because each variable has different p.
    
    # Actually, each variable has its own independent probability p_i. The sum of these Bernoulli variables
    # has a distribution that we can compute via DP.
    
    # Let's implement DP over the variables
    if n == 0:
        return 1.0 if k_percent == 0 else 0.0
    
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1 (so far)
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # initially 0 variables have first digit 1 with probability 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        l, r = variables[i]
        count_1 = count_with_first_digit_1(l, r)
        total_count = r - l + 1
        p = count_1 / total_count if total_count > 0 else 0
        
        # Create new dp array for next state
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # With probability (1-p), the i-th variable doesn't have first digit 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
                # With probability p, the i-th variable has first digit 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all j >= min_required
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(min_required, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    return result

# Handle floating point precision
result = solve()
print(f"{result:.15f}")
```

def count_with_first_digit_1(l, r):
    """Count how many numbers in [l, r] have first digit 1"""
    count = 0
    # Consider all possible powers of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        # Interval [power, 2*power) has first digit 1
        low = max(l, power)
        high = min(r, 2 * power - 1)
        if low <= high:
            count += high - low + 1
        power *= 10
    return count

def combination(n, k):
    """Calculate C(n, k)"""
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    if k == 0 or k == n:
        return 1
    
    # Use smaller of k and n-k for efficiency
    k = min(k, n - k)
    
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result = result * (n - i) // (i + 1)
    return result

def binomial_probability(n, k, p):
    """Calculate P(exactly k successes in n trials with success probability p)"""
    if k > n or k < 0:
        return 0
    if k == 0:
        return (1 - p) ** n
    if k == n:
        return p ** n
    
    # Calculate C(n,k) * p^k * (1-p)^(n-k)
    comb = combination(n, k)
    prob = comb * (p ** k) * ((1 - p) ** (n - k))
    return prob

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k_percent = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability that each variable has first digit 1
    p_values = []
    for l, r in variables:
        count_1 = count_with_first_digit_1(l, r)
        total_count = r - l + 1
        p = count_1 / total_count if total_count > 0 else 0
        p_values.append(p)
    
    # The overall probability is the probability that at least k_percent% of the variables have first digit 1
    # We need at least ceil(k_percent * n / 100) variables to have first digit 1
    min_required = (k_percent * n + 99) // 100  # ceil(k_percent * n / 100)
    
    # Since the variables are independent, we can calculate the probability
    # using the fact that each variable has its own probability p_i of having first digit 1
    # But the events are not identical, so we need to consider the joint distribution
    
    # Actually, we need to calculate the probability that at least min_required variables have first digit 1
    # This requires the distribution of the sum of Bernoulli trials with different success probabilities
    
    # However, this is computationally complex. Let's simplify.
    # The problem likely expects us to assume that all variables have the same probability,
    # or we need to compute the probability more carefully.
    
    # Alternative approach: since the constraints are complex, maybe we're meant to calculate
    # the probability that a randomly selected value has first digit 1 for each variable,
    # and then use binomial distribution with the average probability.
    
    # Actually, let's reconsider. We need the exact probability.
    # We can use dynamic programming to calculate the probability distribution.
    
    # DP state: dp[i][j] = probability that after considering first i variables, exactly j have first digit 1
    # The total number of variables is at most 1000, and we need to track the sum, which can be up to 1000.
    # This should be feasible.
    
    # But note: we don't know the exact distribution because each variable has different p.
    
    # Actually, each variable has its own independent probability p_i. The sum of these Bernoulli variables
    # has a distribution that we can compute via DP.
    
    # Let's implement DP over the variables
    if n == 0:
        return 1.0 if k_percent == 0 else 0.0
    
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1 (so far)
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # initially 0 variables have first digit 1 with probability 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        l, r = variables[i]
        count_1 = count_with_first_digit_1(l, r)
        total_count = r - l + 1
        p = count_1 / total_count if total_count > 0 else 0
        
        # Create new dp array for next state
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # With probability (1-p), the i-th variable doesn't have first digit 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - p)
                # With probability p, the i-th variable has first digit 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * p
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all j >= min_required
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(min_required, n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    return result

# Handle floating point precision
result = solve()

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Nezzar has a binary string s of length n that he wants to share with his best friend, Nanako. Nanako will spend q days inspecting the binary string. At the same time, Nezzar wants to change the string s into string f during these q days, because it looks better.

It is known that Nanako loves consistency so much. On the i-th day, Nanako will inspect a segment of string s from position l_i to position r_i inclusive. If the segment contains both characters '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy and throws away the string.

After this inspection, at the i-th night, Nezzar can secretly change strictly less than half of the characters in the segment from l_i to r_i inclusive, otherwise the change will be too obvious.

Now Nezzar wonders, if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and at the same time have the string become equal to the string f at the end of these q days and nights.

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains two integers n,q (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5, 0 ≤ q ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5).

The second line of each test case contains a binary string s of length n.

The third line of each test case contains a binary string f of length n.

Then q lines follow, i-th of them contains two integers l_i,r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ n) — bounds of the segment, that Nanako will inspect on the i-th day.

It is guaranteed that the sum of n for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5, and the sum of q for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" on the single line if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and have the string f at the end of q days and nights. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

1 3

1111111111

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string `s` that Nezzar wants to transform into string `f` over `q` days.
2. On each day `i`, Nanako inspects a segment `[l_i, r_i]`.
3. If the segment contains both '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy.
4. At night, Nezzar can change strictly less than half of the characters in that segment.
5. We need to determine if it's possible to transform `s` to `f` without Nanako being unhappy.

## Key Insights

1. **Nanako's happiness constraint**: For Nanako to not be unhappy, each segment inspected must be homogeneous (all '0's or all '1's).
2. **Change constraint**: Nezzar can change strictly less than half the characters in each segment.
3. **Goal**: Transform `s` to `f`.

## Analysis

The key insight is that we need to think about consistency constraints from the inspection segments.

For each inspection segment `[l, r]`, at the time of inspection, the segment must be homogeneous (all same characters). After that, Nezzar can change some characters, but strictly less than half of them.

This means that if we have a segment that must be homogeneous at inspection, and we need to change characters within it, the changes are limited.

```python
def solve():
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    s = input().strip()
    f = input().strip()
    
    queries = []
    for _ in range(q):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        queries.append((l, r))
    
    # Check each inspection segment
    for l, r in queries:
        length = r - l + 1
        # Count differences between s and f in segment [l, r]
        differences = 0
        for i in range(l-1, r):
            if s[i] != f[i]:
                differences += 1
        # Can we change strictly less than half of the characters in this segment?
        # We can change at most (length - 1) // 2 characters (strictly less than half)
        if differences > (length - 1) // 2:
            return "NO"
    
    return "YES"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Nezzar has a binary string s of length n that he wants to share with his best friend, Nanako. Nanako will spend q days inspecting the binary string. At the same time, Nezzar wants to change the string s into string f during these q days, because it looks better.

It is known that Nanako loves consistency so much. On the i-th day, Nanako will inspect a segment of string s from position l_i to position r_i inclusive. If the segment contains both characters '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy and throws away the string.

After this inspection, at the i-th night, Nezzar can secretly change strictly less than half of the characters in the segment from l_i to r_i inclusive, otherwise the change will be too obvious.

Now Nezzar wonders, if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and at the same time have the string become equal to the string f at the end of these q days and nights.

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains two integers n,q (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5, 0 ≤ q ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5).

The second line of each test case contains a binary string s of length n.

The third line of each test case contains a binary string f of length n.

Then q lines follow, i-th of them contains two integers l_i,r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ n) — bounds of the segment, that Nanako will inspect on the i-th day.

It is guaranteed that the sum of n for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5, and the sum of q for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" on the single line if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and have the string f at the end of q days and nights. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input


4
5 2
00000
00111
1 5
1 3
2 1
00
01
1 2
10 6
1111111111
0110001110
1 10
5 9
7 10
1 7
3 5
6 10
5 2
10000
11000
2 5
1 3


Output


YES
NO
YES
NO

Note

In the first test case, \underline{00000} → \underline{000}11 → 00111 is one of the possible sequences of string changes.

In the second test case, it can be shown that it is impossible to have the string f at the end.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string `s` that we want to transform into string `f` over `q` days
2. On each day `i`, Nanako inspects a segment from `l_i` to `r_i`
3. If the segment contains both '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy
4. At night, Nezzar can change strictly less than half of the characters in that segment
5. We need to determine if it's possible to reach string `f` without Nanako being unhappy

## Key Insights

1. **Unhappy condition**: If a segment contains both 0 and 1 during inspection, Nanako becomes unhappy. So we must ensure that at the time of inspection, any segment must be uniform (all 0s or all 1s).

2. **Change limitation**: At night, we can change strictly less than half of the characters in a segment. This means:
   - If a segment has length `len`, we can change at most `floor((len-1)/2)` characters
   - This is equivalent to keeping at least `ceil(len/2)` characters unchanged

3. **Strategy**: We need to transform `s` to `f` while ensuring that at each inspection step, the segment is uniform.

4. **Critical observation**: For a segment to be transformable from one state to another, we need to ensure that the required changes don't violate the "strictly less than half" rule.

- At inspection time, the segment must be uniform
- The number of positions that need to change from current state to target state must be achievable with "strictly less than half" changes

```python
    # We can simulate the process:
    # current = list(s)
    # For each inspection segment (in order):
    #   If current[l-1:r] has both 0 and 1, return "NO"
    #   Otherwise, we can change strictly less than half the characters to get closer to f
    #   We change as many positions as possible within the constraint to match f
    
    # But we need to ensure we can reach f eventually.
    
    # Actually, we don't need to simulate exactly. The only impossibility is if at any inspection, the segment is not uniform.
    # And if in any night, we are forced to make more changes than allowed.
    
    # But we can plan the changes optimally.
    
    # The only impossibility is if we need to make changes that exceed the allowed limit in a segment, or if at inspection the segment is not uniform.
    
    # Since we can make changes in multiple nights, and we have multiple opportunities, we only need to ensure that at each inspection, 
    # the segment is uniform.
    
    # But we also need to ensure that we can make the necessary changes to reach f.
    
    # In each night, for a segment [l,r], we can change up to floor((r-l+1-1)/2) = floor((r-l)/2) characters.
    
    # We need to ensure that we can reduce the differences between current and f in each segment.
    
    # This is getting too complex.
    
    # After reading known solutions to similar problems, I believe the solution is:
    
    # For each inspection segment, check if the current string in that segment has both 0 and 1 at the time of inspection.
    # If yes, return "NO".
    # Otherwise, continue.
    # At the end, if we can reach f, return "YES", else "NO".
    
    # But we need to check if we can reach f.
    
    # I think a simpler solution is that it's always possible to reach f if at each inspection the segment is uniform.
    # But example 2 shows it's not.
    
    # In example 2, at inspection [1,2], the segment is "00" which is uniform, but we need to make 1 change, and we can only make 0 changes.
    
    # So we need to check if for each segment, the number of changes needed to reach f in that segment is ≤ floor((length-1)/2)
    
    # But changes happen over multiple nights.
    
    # I think the solution is to simulate the process, but it might be too slow.
    
    # Given the constraints (sum of n, q ≤ 200000), we can simulate.
    
    current = list(s)
    
    for _ in range(q):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        l -= 1  # convert to 0-indexed
        r -= 1
        
        # Check if the segment has both 0 and 1
        segment = current[l:r+1]
        if '0' in segment and '1' in segment:
            print("NO")
            return
        
        # Now, we can change strictly less than half of the characters
        # We want to get closer to f
        seg_target = f[l:r+1]
        
        # Count differences
        diff_count = 0
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != seg_target[i]:
                diff_count += 1
                
        # We can change at most floor((r-l+1-1)/2) = floor((r-l)/2) characters
        max_changes = (r - l) // 2  # strictly less than half, so floor((len-1)/2) = (len-1)//2
        
        # If we need to make more changes than allowed, we can't do it in one night
        # But we have future nights to make changes
        # We can make changes in future nights as well
        
        # The only constraint is that at each inspection, the segment must be uniform
        # We can plan the changes to make fewer changes over time
        
        # For now, we only need to make progress toward f
        # We can change up to max_changes positions to match f
        
        # Change as many as possible
        # We'll greedily change to match f
        changes_made = 0
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != seg_target[i]:
                if changes_made < max_changes:
                    current[i] = seg_target[i]
                    changes_made += 1
                else:
                    # Cannot make more changes, break
                    break
    
    # After all inspections, check if we reached f
    if ''.join(current) == f:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    solve()
```

Wait, I see a problem with my simulation. I'm not processing the inspection segments in order properly. I need to process each inspection in sequence.

```python
def solve():
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    s = input().strip()
    f = input().strip()
    
    current = list(s)
    
    # Process each inspection

        r -= 1

        if '0' in segment and '1' in segment:

        # Length of segment
        seg_len = r - l + 1
        max_changes = (seg_len - 1) // 2  # strictly less than half
        
        # Get the target for this segment
        target_segment = f[l:r+1]
        
        # Count how many positions need to change
        diff_count = 0
        changes_made = 0
        
        # Make changes greedily to match target
        for i in range(l, r+1):

                    current[i] = target_segment[i]
```

But this might not be correct because we're not using the fact that changes can be made in multiple nights. We need to ensure that the final state is f.

Actually, the above simulation should work because:
1. We ensure that at each inspection, the segment is uniform
2. We make changes to get closer to f in each night, within the allowed constraints

- seg_len = 2, max_changes = (2-1)//2 = 0

1. [1,5] 
2. [1,3]

After first night: change positions 2,3 (0->1) to get "00110"
Then inspection [1,3]: positions 1,2,3: "001" which has both 0 and 1 -> "NO"

Let me check the example: 
"00000 → 00011 → 00111"

On first night: after inspection [1,5], we change to 00011 (positions 4,5)
On second night: after inspection [1,3], we change position 3 from 0 to 1.

But after the first change, we have "00011", so positions 1,2,3 are "000", which is uniform at inspection [1,3].

So we need to change only positions 4,5 in the first night, not 2,3.

So in the first inspection [1,5], we can change strictly less than half of 5 characters, which is less than 2.5, so at most 2 changes.

We can change positions 4,5 from 0 to 1, getting "00011".

Then at inspection [1,3]: segment is "000" (positions 1,2,3) which is uniform.

Then at night, we can change strictly less than half of 3 characters (less than 1.5), so at most 1 character.

We change position 3 from 0 to 1, getting "00111".

So in the simulation, for the first night, we should change positions 4,5.

In the code, when we have segment "00000" and target "00111", we need to change positions 2,3,4 (0-indexed 2,3,4).

We can make at most 2 changes. So we should change only 2 of them.

The greedy approach of changing as many as possible might not work because we want to preserve uniformity for future inspections.

I see the issue. The greedy approach of changing as many positions as needed might create non-uniform segments in future inspections.

We need to be more strategic. We might want to only change positions that won't create non-uniform segments in future inspections.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    s = input().strip()
    f = input().strip()
    
    current = list(s)
    
    valid = True
    for _ in range(q):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        l -= 1
        r -= 1
        
        # Check if the segment has both 0 and 1
        segment = current[l:r+1]
        if '0' in segment and '1' in segment:
            valid = False
            break
            
        # Calculate how many changes we can make
        length = r - l + 1
        max_changes = (length - 1) // 2
        
        # Count mismatches with target
        target = f[l:r+1]
        mismatches = 0
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != target[i]:
                mismatches += 1
                
        # We can only change max_changes positions
        # So we can only make changes within that limit
        # We change as many as possible
        changes_made = 0
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != target[i]:
                if changes_made < max_changes:
                    current[i] = target[i]
                    changes_made += 1
                else:
                    break
    
    if valid:
        if ''.join(current) == f:
            print("YES")
        else:
            print("NO")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    s = input().strip()
    f = input().strip()
    
    current = list(s)
    possible = True
    
    for _ in range(q):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        l -= 1
        r -= 1
        
        # Check if the segment has both 0 and 1 at inspection time
        segment = current[l:r+1]
        if '0' in segment and '1' in segment:
            possible = False
            break
            
        # Calculate how many changes we can make (strictly less than half)
        length = r - l + 1
        max_changes = (length - 1) // 2
        
        # Target for this segment
        target_segment = f[l:r+1]
        
        # Count how many positions differ from target
        diff_count = 0
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != target_segment[i]:
                diff_count += 1
                
        # We can only change up to max_changes positions
        changes_to_make = min(diff_count, max_changes)
        
        # Make the changes
        for i in range(l, r+1):
            if current[i] != target_segment[i] and changes_to_make > 0:
                current[i] = target_segment[i]
                changes_to_make -= 1
    
    if possible and ''.join(current) == f:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Nezzar has a binary string s of length n that he wants to share with his best friend, Nanako. Nanako will spend q days inspecting the binary string. At the same time, Nezzar wants to change the string s into string f during these q days, because it looks better.

It is known that Nanako loves consistency so much. On the i-th day, Nanako will inspect a segment of string s from position l_i to position r_i inclusive. If the segment contains both characters '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy and throws away the string.

After this inspection, at the i-th night, Nezzar can secretly change strictly less than half of the characters in the segment from l_i to r_i inclusive, otherwise the change will be too obvious.

Now Nezzar wonders, if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and at the same time have the string become equal to the string f at the end of these q days and nights.

Input

The first line contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains two integers n,q (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5, 0 ≤ q ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5).

The second line of each test case contains a binary string s of length n.

The third line of each test case contains a binary string f of length n.

Then q lines follow, i-th of them contains two integers l_i,r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ n) — bounds of the segment, that Nanako will inspect on the i-th day.

It is guaranteed that the sum of n for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5, and the sum of q for all test cases doesn't exceed 2 ⋅ 10^5.

Output

For each test case, print "YES" on the single line if it is possible to avoid Nanako being unhappy and have the string f at the end of q days and nights. Otherwise, print "NO".

You can print each letter in any case (upper or lower).

Example

Input


4
5 2
00000
00111
1 5
1 3
2 1
00
01
1 2
10 6
1111111111
0110001110
1 10
5 9
7 10
1 7
3 5
6 10
5 2
10000
11000
2 5
1 3


Output


YES
NO
YES
NO

Note

In the first test case, \underline{00000} → \underline{000}11 → 00111 is one of the possible sequences of string changes.

In the second test case, it can be shown that it is impossible to have the string f at the end.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string `s` that we want to transform into string `f` over `q` days.
2. On each day `i`, Nanako inspects a segment `[l_i, r_i]`.
3. If the segment contains both '0' and '1', Nanako becomes unhappy.
4. At night, Nezzar can change strictly less than half of the characters in that segment.
5. We need to determine if it's possible to reach string `f` without Nanako ever being unhappy.

## Key Insights

1. **Unhappiness condition**: Nanako is unhappy if a segment has both '0' and '1'. So we must ensure that during every inspection, the segment has only one type of character (all 0s or all 1s).

2. **Change constraint**: Nezzar can change strictly less than half of the characters. This means:
   - In a segment of length `L`, he can change at most `(L-1)//2` characters.
   - This implies that after changes, at least half of the characters must remain unchanged.

3. **Strategy**: We need to think about when we can make changes. The key insight is that we can only make changes when the segment is homogeneous (all 0s or all 1s). After changes, the segment might become heterogeneous, but we must avoid making it heterogeneous during inspections.

4. **Odd/even analysis**: The constraint "strictly less than half" means we can only change characters when there's an odd number of characters or when we have a specific pattern.

5. **Critical observation**: For a segment to be changed while maintaining homogeneity, we need to think about parity. The constraint "less than half" means we can't change more than half.

6. **Final insight**: The key is to work backwards or consider the compatibility of changes. We need to check if we can transform `s` to `f` such that at each inspection segment, we never introduce both 0s and 1s.

Actually, let me reconsider with a simpler approach:

## Simpler Approach

The core constraint is that during any inspection `[l, r]`, the segment must have only one type of character (all 0s or all 1s). So at any point, for every inspection interval, all characters in that interval must be the same.

The change operation allows us to flip strictly less than half of the characters. This means that if a segment has length L, we can flip at most floor((L-1)/2) characters.

This gives us a key property: if we have a segment with both 0s and 1s, we cannot fix it by flipping less than half the characters - because we'd need to flip exactly the mismatched characters, and that might exceed the limit.
